CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION The Tenth Semiannual Report of the Small Business 

Administration provides detailed information on the progress of the Agency in carrying out these responsibilities assigned to it by Congress. 

1. 2. 

Helping small business gain access to adequate capital and credit;

Helping small business obtain a fair share of Government procurement; 3. 

Helping small business obtain competent management, technical, and production counsel; and 

4. Making disaster loans to persons whose homes or businesses have been damaged by storms, floods, and other disasters, and to small business concerns which have suffered substantial economic injury because of excessive rainfall or drought conditions in their areas. 

In addition, this report reviews various aspects of the changing economic position of small business in our economy. 

During the period covered by this report, the Agency's 

Financial Assistance and Procurement Assistance Programs both reached record levels. In this 6-months period, 4, 698 business loan applications requesting more than $257 million were received, and 2, 513 business loans for $122 million were approved. This compares with 3, 527 applications for $189 million and 1, 936 business loan approvals for $89 million in the same 6-months period of 1957. Government purchases set aside for small business in 1

2 the fiscal year which ended June 30, 1958, totaled more than $1 billion. This was the greatest dollar amount of set-asides in any fiscal year since the start of the Agency and was an increase of more than $300 million over the set-asides in the prior fiscal year. 

The set-aside program resulted last fiscal year in 18, 149 contract awards, totaling $677 million, to small business concerns, as compared with 13, 416 contract awards for $553 million in the previous fiscal year.

Many activities of this Agency during the past 6-months period were conducted in co-operation with other Government agencies, with the Small Business Administration serving as spokesman and advocate of small business. 

The Administrator, in addition to serving as Chairman of the Small Business Administration's Loan Policy Board, is a member of the President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business, the Defense and Civilian Mobilization Board, the Committee for 

Rural Development Program and the Board of Directors of the Virgin Islands Corporation. 

The Small Business Administration is represented also on the following interagency committees:

Subcommittees of President's Cabinet Committee on

Specific examples of Small Business Administration collaboration with other agencies are as follows:

This Agency has worked continuously with the Council of Economic Advisers on studies of programs for assistance to small business.

Concerning methods and means of making equity capital more readily available to small business, this Agency has worked closely with the Securities and Exchange Commission. 

Representatives of this Agency have conferred with representatives of the Civil Aeronautics Administration and the 

Civil Aeronautics Board about financial assistance to small airlines, and loans to private business concerns for hangar facilities. 

Questions of subscription television and its impact on small business concerns have been discussed with members of the Federal Communications Commission. 

The Agency has continued co-operation with officials of the Fish and Wildlife Service of the Department of the Interior in administering the Fisheries Loan Program. Loan applications under this program are processed and approved loans disbursed and administered by the Small Business Administration. 

The Small Business Administration Liaison Office with the Department of Defense makes a continuous study of current defense procurement policies and their effect on small business and recommends changes which would be beneficial to small concerns.

The Small Business Administration has established a continuing liaison with the Antitrust Division of the Department of Justice through which the Agency has obtained lists of inventions and patents made available for licensing to the public through judgments in antitrust cases. The Agency has published abstracts of these patents in the monthly Products List Circular distributed to small business concerns. Officials of this Agency also have been working closely with the Department of Justice in both the development of antitrust legislation and the handling of specific complaints from small business under the antitrust laws.

The Agency has collaborated with the Business and Defense Services Administration of the Department of Commerce in helping small business concerns obtain access to scarce materials. 

In the Disaster Loan Program the Small Business Ad ministration has worked in close co-operation with the Federal 

Civil Defense Administration, the Housing and Home Finance 

Agency, the Department of Agriculture and the American Red Cross to provide assistance to the victims of disasters. 

Conferences have been held with officials of the Patent Office regarding fees for the filing of patents and general Government patent policy.

With regard to small business participation in the President's highway program, the Agency has worked with officials of the Bureau of Public Roads in the Department of Commerce. 

The Agency has conferred with the Bureau of Foreign Commerce in the Department of Commerce in regard to the effect of high tariffs on small business concerns and has brought to the attention of the United States Tariff Commission the effect on small businesses of increases in tariffs. 

The Agency has continued to confer with representatives of the Department of Labor in regard to amendments to existing labor laws which might improve the labor-management relations of small business concerns. 

Recommendations have been made to the Department of State about hardships imposed on small business in cases such as the export of ferrous scrap and other materials in short supply, and restrictions imposed on goods shipped from the United States. Conferences have been held with various Departments and Bureaus, including the Department of Defense and the Bureau of the Census, on methods of improving the definition of small business.

Conferences have continued with representatives of the Urban Renewal Administration to discuss possible joint assistance programs for small business concerns displaced by new housing programs.

Many Federal agencies have co-operated in the Business Opportunity Meetings cosponsored by this Agency to inform small business owners and managers of opportunities to sell to the Government and to obtain subcontract work. To date, 46 such meetings have been held of which 9 were held during the 6-months period from January through June 1958. Agencies co-operating in this work include various installations of the Army, Navy, Air Force and the joint military supply agencies, as well as such Federal civilian agencies as the Department of Commerce, General Services Ad ministration, Post Office Department, Department of the Interior and Veterans Administration. 

This Agency has brought to the attention of the Department of Justice and the Federal Trade Commission instances of possible violation of the antitrust laws and the Federal Trade 

Commission Act. 

Conferences have been held with the Departments of Commerce, Defense and Labor about a revision of the definition of 

"Regular Dealer" under the Walsh-Healey Public Contracts Act. 

This Agency in collaboration with other agencies, has reviewed the labor standards provisions of Government construction contracts in an effort to simplify the procedures and thus facilitate the participation of small business in Government procurement. 

A member of this Agency serves on the Surplus Labor Board, which deals with problems of surplus manpower. Other agencies represented on the Board are the military and civilian procurement agencies and the Department of Labor and the Office of Defense and Civilian Mobilization. 

The Small Business Administration is collaborating with the General Services Administration, the Department of Defense, and other agencies in a study of Government procedures, policies, and regulations which adversely affect small business. 

Agency employees are participating with the General Services Administration in the preparation of a single set of Government procurement regulations.

The Small Business Administration serves with the 

Department of Commerce, the General Services Administration and the Office of Defense and Civilian Mobilization in developing policies for leasing of Government machine tools.

This Agency has co-operated with the Federal Reserve Board in studies on the financing of small business. In connection with legislation which was under consideration this period by both Houses of Congress the Agency studied methods of providing small business longer term loans and equity capital. Two research studies commissioned by this Agency were helpful in Congressional consideration of these measures one study dealt with State development corporations, the other with local development corporations in the State of Minnesota, where such corporations have operated successfully. These studies were published by the Senate Committee on 

Banking and Currency in the committee print, "Development Corporations and Authorities."

During the past 6 months, delegations from many European, Asiatic and South American countries visited the Small Business Administration's Washington office. Composed mainly of small businessmen, government officials, bankers and executives of small industry, these delegations were provided detailed information on the various services of the Small Business Administration. The visitors were particularly interested in the Financial 

Assistance Program, the production and technical assistance activities, and the economic status of small business in the United States. In some instances, the visitors expressed an intention of trying to enlist support for similar small business programs in their own countries.

CHAPTER II ECONOMICS OF SMALL BUSINESS 

This chapter presents data which indicate the economic position of small business, such as the number of new business incorporations, the number of business failures, the earnings of smaller manufacturing corporations, military prime contract awards to small business and financial factors affecting small business.

Business Failures 

Through June 30, 1958, Dun and Bradstreet, Inc., had reported 8, 071 business failures in 1958, compared with 7, 089 failures in the like period of 1957. After reaching a postwar high of 1, 495 in March 1958, the number of failures began to decline, dropping to 1, 458 in April, 1, 341 in May, and 1, 260 in June.

It is important to note the industries in which failures increased in the first half of 1958. 

In the construction industry, where failures in 1957 were substantially higher than in 1956, the first half of 1958 saw little change in the number of failures among building subcontractors. However, failures among general building contractors increased, reflecting the earlier decline in residential construction. 

Failures also increased in the first half of 1958 in these industries and trades: Lumber and lumber products; paper; printing and publishing; metalworking; retailers of furniture and home furnishings; retailers of apparel and accessories; and the automotive group, which includes filling stations and motor vehicle and other automotive dealers.

The marked rise in failures in the construction field is understandable in light of the tremendous increase that has occurred in the number of construction firms. From 244, 000 construction businesses in 1946, the number had grown to 477, 000 by 1957. In

The number of business failures by years is shown on page 10 and the yearly rates of business failure on page 11.

New Business Incorporations 

The number of business incorporations is a useful indicator of business growth because a significant relationship exists between new business incorporations and new business formations. 

Furthermore, figures on incorporations are available on a more current basis than statistics on new businesses. The usefulness of the figures on incorporations as a measure of business growth is lessened by the fact that they include both incorporations of new businesses and incorporations of established businesses which previously had been operated as proprietorships or partnerships. The number of new business incorporations in the first 6 months of 1958 was 2 per cent under the same months of 1957. The postwar trend of new business incorporations is shown in the chart on page 12. 

Financial Position of Smaller Manufacturing Corporations 

Sales In the first quarter of 1958, sales of the smaller manufacturing corporations (those with assets under $1 million) were $10, 226 million, almost the same as in the first quarter of 1957, when they were $10, 326 million. In the last quarter of 1957, sales of smaller manufacturing corporations totaled $10, 888 million, as compared with the last quarter 1956 figure of $11, 152 million. 

In the case of the larger manufacturing corporations (those with assets over $1 million) the decline in sales was much sharper. 

In the first quarter of 1958, the larger corporations' sales were $62, 260 million substantially below their sales of $69, 323 million in the first quarter of 1957. The sharper decline in the sales of these larger corporations was due primarily to the reduced operations of manufacturers of automobiles and other durable goods. The chart on page 15 shows the trend of net sales by asset size of corporations in the years 1951-57. The base year for these indexes is 1951. 483267 O-58-2

Earnings 

Although sales of the smaller manufacturing corporations were about the same in 1957 as in 1956, earnings of corporations both before and after taxes decreased significantly, due to increased costs and expenses. Earnings before taxes of the smaller corporations decreased from $1, 846 million in 1956 to $1, 458 million in 1957. Larger manufacturing corporations also experienced a decrease in earnings. Their earnings before taxes declined from $27, 922 million in 1956 to $26, 709 million in 1957. This decrease continued into the first quarter 1958 in the case of both smaller and large manufacturing corporations. Earnings before taxes of the smaller corporations dropped to $135 million compared with $362 million in the first quarter 1957. For the larger corporations they dropped to $4, 526 million from $7, 372 million in the first quarter a year ago.

The trend of earnings after taxes was similar. Earnings after taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations decreased from $985 million in 1956 to $724 million in 1957. The decrease for the larger corporations was from $15, 168 million in 1956 to $14, 714 million in 1957. In the first quarter of 1958, the drop in earnings after taxes was even sharper, compared with the first quarter of 1957. They fell from $181 million to $10 million for the smaller manufacturing corporations, and from $3, 918 million to $2, 462 million for the larger manufacturing corporations. The earnings of smaller manufacturing corporations show a traditional pattern of wide fluctuation, while the earnings of the larger manufacturing corporations tend to have more stability.

In the table on page 14, figures are presented for earnings by asset size before and after taxes for 1951-57. The chart on page 15 shows the trend of earnings by asset size before and after taxes for the same years.

Rate of Return on Stockholders' Equity 

Stockholders' equity in a corporation is comprised of capital stock, minority interest, earned surplus, surplus reserves, and that portion of other reserves which is considered surplus. 

The table on page 16 presents the annual rate of earnings on stockholders' equity by asset size of business before and

after taxes. In the chart on page 17, the trends of the annual rates of return on stockholders' equity are shown by asset size for the period 1947-57. 

For the full year 1957, the rate of return on stockholders' equity in smaller manufacturing corporations before taxes was 15.3 per cent, and after taxes, 7.6 per cent. In the case of the larger corporations, the return before taxes was 20.3 per cent, and after taxes, 11.2 per cent. In the first quarter of 1958, small businesses showed a sharply diminished return. Before taxes their rate of return was 5.5 per cent, and after taxes, 0.4 per cent. The rate of return for the larger corporations before taxes was 13.4 per cent and after taxes 7.3 per cent.

From 1947 to 1957 the stockholders' equity in the smaller manufacturing corporations increased from $5, 894 million to $9,553 million -- an increase of 62 per cent. In the same period, the stockholders' equity in larger corporations increased from $59, 178 million to $131, 513 million an increase of 122 per cent.

Military Procurement 

Military prime contract awards to small business in the first 11 months of fiscal year 1958 totaled $2,770 million. This was 16.2 per cent of the $17, 146 million net value of all military prime contract awards in this period. In the first 11 months of fiscal year 1957, military prime contract awards to small business totaled $3, 175 million, or 18.9 per cent of the $16, 824 million of net military prime contract awards in that period.

The decrease in the percentage and dollar value of awards to small business in the first 11 months of fiscal 1958, as compared with the same period of fiscal 1957, resulted from changes in the kinds of weapons and other material purchased by the military establishment. In general, there was a decrease in construction, textiles, clothing and equipage, and other programs which provide major opportunities for small business participation in prime contract awards, and, conversely, there was a significant increase in the proportion of awards for aircraft, guided missiles and other complex weapons whose development and production small business is not equipped to undertake under prime contracts. As shown in the table on page 20, the net value of all military purchasing from business firms in the United States was

Of the 13 categories of procurement shown in the table on page 23, 5 showed increases in the small business percentages while 8 showed decreases. Generally, programs which increased in dollar volume showed a decrease in the small business percentage.

As indicated in another section of this report, the Small 

Business Administration-Department of Defense small business set-aside program improved in effectiveness during the year. 

This type of assistance was particularly evident in the increased small business participation in the fuel and lubricants program, from 24 per cent in 1957 to 30.7 per cent in fiscal 1958. 

Small Business Share of New and Net Procurement 

The small business share of net military procurement over the past 3 fiscal years has been about 2 per cent greater than its share of new procurement. "New procurement" refers to all new contracts plus contract changes that increase the amount of obligations by $10, 000 or more. "Net procurement" refers to "new procurement" less contract modifications that decrease the amount of obligations by $10, 000 or more. 

The small business share of net military procurement is higher because of the fact that downward contract modifications have been relatively more for large than for small business. 

The table on page 20 shows the small business share of the value of military contract awards, on both a new and net procurement basis, for the months of July through May of fiscal years 1957 and 1958. Data are given on both total military purchasing and purchases by the individual services. The table on page 21 gives the small business and large business percentages of military prime contract awards in the same periods of fiscal 1957 and 1958. 

Again, figures are given both for total military purchasing and purchases by the individual services.

The trend of military contract awards to small business in relation to total contract awards for both new and net procurement, beginning with the first quarter of fiscal year 1955 and continuing through the third quarter of fiscal year 1958, is shown in the table on page 26 and in the chart on page 27.

Small Business Potential 

The small business potential includes all military procurement actions of $10,000 or more for which there are known small business sources and which can be offered to small business after considering such factors as patent rights, delivery schedules, interchangeability of parts, etc. In addition, for the purposes of reporting on military prime contract awards to small business and other contractors, it is assumed that all procurement actions of less than $10, 000 also are in the small business potential. Since this assumption is not strictly correct, the reports to this extent overstate the small business potential and understate the share of the potential awarded to small business. 

The small business potential of military procurement actions for work in the United States during the period July 1957 through May 1958 was $4, 472 million, or 26.1 per cent of the total net value of such procurement actions. This compares with $5, 396 million or 32. 1 per cent in the same period last year.

The dollar amounts and the percentages within the small business potential for each of the military services in the periods July 1956 through May 1957 and July 1957 through May 1958 were as follows:

Small Business Share of Advertised and Negotiated 

Contracts 

Where military purchases are within the small business potential, small business receives about the same percentage of awards regardless of whether the purchases are made by advertised or negotiated procurement. In the period July 1957 through May 1958, the small business share of advertised procurement which was within the small business potential was 61.1 per cent, of negotiated procurement 62.6 per cent. The net value of advertised procurement actions placed with small business firms in this period was $1,239 million, or 53.7 per cent of total advertised military procurement actions. The net value of negotiated military procurement actions with small business firms was $1,531 million, or 10. 3 per cent of all negotiated procurement actions. The higher small business percentage in the case of advertised procurement is due to the fact that a greater portion of advertised procurement is within the small business potential.

Financial Factors Affecting Loan Applications 

The Federal Reserve Board conducted a questionnaire survey of business loans by member banks as of October 16, 1957. This survey was comparable to a previous one conducted as of October 5, 1955. The results of the most recent survey were published in a report to Congress by the Federal Reserve System on April 11, 1958.

This survey showed that although business loans as a whole were greatly expanded during the period 1955-57, small businesses did not share in this expansion of credit as fully as did the larger concerns. 

Loans outstanding by asset size of borrower changed as follows:

Between 1946 and 1955, loans to concerns with assets under $50,000 declined from 65 to 46 per cent of the number of loans outstanding. Between 1955 and 1957 they declined further to 39. 4 per cent.

From the standpoint of dollar amount, between 1946 and 1955 loans to concerns with assets under $50, 000 declined from 9 per cent to 6 per cent of loans outstanding, and between 1955 and 

1957 they declined further to 3.6 per cent.

In recent months, despite various steps which the Federal Reserve System has taken to increase the availability of credit by cutting reserve requirements and lowering the discount rates, outstanding loans of weekly reporting member banks of the Federal Reserve System have been declining. In September 1957, outstanding loans of weekly reporting member banks of the Federal Reserve System totaled $31.9 billion. By January 1958 they had dropped to $30. 7 billion. Despite the normal seasonal increase this spring, at the end of June the outstanding loans totaled only $29.6 billion, a drop of $1.9 billion compared with last year.

Equity Capital for Small Business 

The Federal Reserve Board conducted an extensive survey as to the adequacy of long-term funds for small business, and presented its findings to Congress in April 1958. In summing up the 

Board's findings, Dr. George Garvy of the Federal Reserve Bank said that there was evidence of "an unfilled margin, perhaps a rather thin one, between the volume of funds available to small concerns in general, and to new firms in particular, and the volume that could be put to use without prohibitive risk. It is also possible that this margin varies with changes in credit and business conditions. " An outstanding authority on small business, Dr. A. D. H. 

Kaplan, in the study, "Adequacy of Small Business Financing: One

View," stated, "The greatest need is for an institution to which small business can bring its need for capital. There is no opportunity for small business to outline its plans for growth indicating the type of capital it requires and secure advice on and consideration of its plans. Small business could think in this manner if there were a point in so doing. " On the basis of the Federal Reserve Board study, the Congress during this period considered extensively a bill which would authorize the Small Business Administration to assist small business investment companies and local and State development 483267 O-58-3

corporations. These companies and corporations in turn would serve as intermediate credit institutions for the long-term and equity capital needs of small business. 

Thus far in 1958 there has been a continuation in the decline in small securities issues that was evident in 1957. Total corporate securities offerings in the first 5 months of 1958 were only slightly lower than those in the comparable period of 1957, $5, 299 million as against $5, 320 million. However, securities issues under Regulation A, through which the Securities and Exchange Commission provides a simplified registration procedure for issues between $100, 000 and $300, 000, fell to $41 million as against $49 million in 1957, and were only 0.8 per cent of total corporate securities offerings.

CHAPTER III FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE Highlights of the Agency's Financial Assistance Program in the 6-months period covered by this report, January through June 

1958, are as follows: 

1. 2. 3.

The Agency's disaster loan authority was expanded significantly through Congression al authorization of Small Business Adminis tration disaster loans to small business concerns which have suffered economic injury because of excessive rainfall.

The following table summarizes the business lending activity of the Small Business Administration in the period covered by this report, in the comparable period of 1957, and from the start of the Agency through June 30, 1958.

In this reporting period, the Small Business Administration approved 2, 513 business loans totaling $121, 634, 000. This brought total business loan approvals in fiscal year 1958 to 4, 014 for $194,997,000.

By comparison, in the first 6 months of 1957, the Agency approved 1, 936 business loans for $89, 036, 000 and in the entire fiscal year 1957, it approved 3, 536 business loans for $159, 095, 000. 

These previously were the record highs in the Business Loan Program. 

In January-June 1958 the Small Business Administration received 4, 698 business loan applications requesting a total of $257, 402,000. The previous record period was January-June 1957 when 3, 527 applications for a total of $188, 916, 000 were filed with the Agency.

In the 6 months covered by this report, the Small Business 

Administration approved 792 disaster loans totaling $11, 606, 000.

In the entire fiscal year 1958 the Agency approved 1, 559 disaster loans for $17, 306, 000. In January-June 1957 the Agency approved 

1, 319 disaster loans for $10, 361, 000, and in the entire 1957 fiscal year, it approved 1,597 disaster loans for $12,992, 000.

Administration of Loan Program 

In order to provide faster service on loan applications, the Small Business Administration has made various changes in its loan procedures and has delegated additional lending authority to its regional offices. 

The Agency has recently amended its requirements to simplify the procedures for making small direct loans.

During this reporting period, the Agency also moved to speed up closing of loans of $20,000 or less by reducing the number of required loan conditions which borrowers formerly had to meet.

Staff members in the field offices have been given additional authority to approve loans and to take action in regard to the servicing of loans.

The Small Business Administration is now servicing approximately 14, 700 business and disaster loans, including loans

transferred from the former Reconstruction Finance Corporation. 

With the increase in Small Business Administration loan approvals, it is inevitable that the servicing and administration of loans will become a still more important job of the Agency. In the Agency's regional offices, which service and administer the loans, emphasis is being placed on careful analysis of the repayment records of all borrowers so that preventive action can be taken, if necessary, before a loan becomes delinquent. The Agency's financial specialists are: 1. 2. 3. 

Providing more counseling to borrowers in all matters affecting the operations of their businesses;

Obtaining more frequent financial statements from borrowers, for careful examination; and 

Increasing the number of visits they make to a borrower's plant, to inspect his facilities and equipment.

Financial Counseling 

Small Business Administration financial specialists who have had long experience in credit and financial matters are available to counsel with small business owners and managers on their problems.

These specialists explore carefully each request for financial assistance to see if the small firm's problem can be solved without recourse to Government financial aid. Frequently, through helpful suggestions, such as for changes in capitalization of the business, liquidation of outdated or surplus inventory, or modernization of sales methods, the financial specialists are able to help the small business overcome its difficulties without borrowing. Where borrowing apparently is the solution, the specialists often are able to help the business obtain the loan from private lending sources. When equity capital is the solution, the businessman is referred to investment bankers.

On some occasions, after the Small Business Administration has processed a loan to the stage of closing and disbursement, a bank which originally declined the small concern's request has agreed to make the loan. Such a result, while producing no interest

In some instances it is evident that a loan would not be the solution to a businessman's financial problems, or that his application would not meet reasonable credit standards. Although the businessman is advised of the facts in these cases, it is the Agency's policy not to discourage the filing of any application. 

Therefore, if the businessman wishes to file his application, the Agency accepts it and gives it earnest consideration. 

Applications and Approvals 

During the latest 6-months period, the Agency received a record number of business loan applications 4,698 request- ing a total of $257, 402,000. In May, an all-time high of 1, 099 applications for $57, 344, 000 was received. In June, applications continued at a high level -- 845 were received, requesting a total

of $43, 150, 000. This trend of increased applications is in contrast to the trend of decreasing applications for loans in the private banking field.

The table on page 34 summarizes the Agency's business loan activity during the past 3 fiscal years. As indicated in the accompanying chart, there has been a seasonal trend in the volume of loan applications each fiscal year, with applications tending to increase in the January-June period, as compared with the July- 

December period. 

Approvals and Declines 

The ratio of approvals to loan applications acted on increased very sharply in this 6-months period. This is illustrated by the chart on page 35. The ratio of 64.9 per cent compares with 

52.4 per cent in the previous period and 58. 8 per cent for the same period in 1957.

The 64.9 per cent ratio of approvals to applications acted on in this period compares with a cumulative ratio of approvals of 53.9 per cent since the beginning of the Agency's business lending operations. 

Bank Participation

Sixty per cent of the business loans approved by the Small Business Administration in this period were bank participation loans. 

The ratio of loan approvals of this type to total loan approvals continues to decline as shown in the table and chart on page 37; in the previous 6-months period 66 per cent of approved loans were bank participation loans.

In the last 2 years, a growing number of banks have asked the Agency to participate in loans on an immediate rather than a deferred basis. This shift to immediate participation loans may reflect a desire of the banks to use their loan funds to extend credit to as many customers as possible. It also may reflect the relative unprofitability to banks of deferred participation loans, in view of the higher interest rates which they may obtain on loans made without Agency participation.

Despite the continuing decrease in the percentage of participation loans, loans of this type continue to account for a major part

of the loan approvals, and are mutually advantageous to the small business owner, the participating institution, and the Government. 

A bank participation loan gives the small business borrower the opportunity to establish and maintain a satisfactory credit record with the bank -- a record which will be of prime importance to him in years to come. From the standpoint of the bank, the participation plan affords an opportunity to make a longer term -- and sometimes a larger loan than would otherwise be possible. Further, it permits the bank to broaden its lending activity while maintaining liquidity, obtaining a fair return on money loaned, and providing increased service to its customers. From the standpoint of the Small 

Business Administration, the plan reduces loan collection and servicing costs, requires fewer Government employees, lessens the drain on lending funds, and may make possible sale of an entire loan, when seasoned, to the participating bank.

Bank Participation Fees Effective April 1, 1958, the Small Business Administra tion's Loan Policy Board reduced by 50 per cent the fees charged banks for Agency participation in loans on a deferred basis. 

The new rates were made applicable not only to deferred participation loans made after that date, but also to loans of that type already on the books. Undoubtedly, this reduction in the Agency's fee for deferred participation loans will enhance the attractiveness of deferred participation loans to banks. 

The Board also approved a service fee for banks which service loans in which the Small Business Administration participates on an immediate basis. The fee amounts to 1/2 of 1 per cent per annum on the unpaid principal balance of the Government's portion of the loan.

The Loan Policy Board also has ruled that a participating bank may make a service charge to a borrower for the handling of a construction loan, provided the bank shows that extraordinary servicing is involved and that the fee does not represent additional interest or profit on the loan. The maximum permissible charge is 2 per cent on the outstanding balance of the loan. 

Limited Loan Participations The Limited Loan Participation Program was designed especially to assist small retail, whole sale, and service establishments (other types of business also are eligible). A concern of this type may be unable to pledge as much tangible collateral as is required for regular business loans, but may have a favorable earnings, management and credit record and therefore is deserving of consideration.

In this program, the Small Business Administration will join with a bank in a loan to a small firm up to a maximum of $15,000, or 75 per cent of the loan, whichever is the smaller. During the last 

6 months, 28.5 per cent of the business loans approved by the Agency were of this type.

As shown in the table on page 38, most of the loans have been to retailers, wholesalers, and service establishments, for which this program was chiefly designed. In the last 6 months,

almost 64 per cent of the loans under this plan were to firms in these lines. By comparison, 48 per cent of all Agency business loans approved through June 30, 1958, were to such firms. 

On a cumulative basis, through June 30, 1958, loans to retail establishments had accounted for 47 per cent of total loan approvals in the Limited Loan Participation Program, and loans to manufacturers, 21 per cent. The comparable percentages for the over-all business loan program were 29 per cent for retail establishments and 36 per cent for manufacturing establishments.

Characteristics of Business Loans Approved 

Size of Loans 

The 4, 698 business loan applications received by the 

Small Business Administration in the period January-June 1958 

requested a total of $257, 402, 000, indicating an average size of loan requested of $54, 790. This average is less than the average of $59, 600 in the preceding 6-months period, when 3, 200 applications for $190, 709, 000 were received. As to loans approved, the average size in this period was $48, 402, a slight reduction in the previous averages.

This reflects the increased effectiveness of the Limited Loan Participation Program, and the Agency's action in simplifying the procedures for making smaller direct loans.

Loans approved on an immediate participation basis during this period averaged $54, 387. Deferred participation loans averaged $43, 354, and direct loans averaged $43, 540.

As shown in the table on page 40, 57 per cent of the business loans approved in the period under review were for $25,000 or less, and nearly 73 per cent were for $50, 000 or less. 

Size of Business 

The size pattern of manufacturers for whom loans were approved in the period January-June 1958 also reflects the emphasis on smaller loans. In this period, as shown on page 42, of the business loans approved for manufacturers, 563, or 77 per cent were to companies with fewer than 50 employees. Only 67 loans, or less than 10 per cent were to manufacturers with 100 or more employees.

Nonmanufacturers are classified by sales size, rather than by number of employees. In the last 6-months period, 62 per cent of the 1, 650 loans approved for nonmanufacturers were to firms with less than $100, 000 annual sales volume, while 44 per cent were to concerns having an annual sales volume of less than $50,000. This compares with 50 and 31 per cent in the preceding 6-months period.

Loans to retailers now account for 29 per cent of the total business loans by number. In the retail field, loans to building materials, hardware, and farm implement dealers account for the largest portion of loans. 

Next in number of loans are automobile dealers and filling stations and food stores. Loans to service establishments account for about 10 per cent of the total business loans by number.

The interval between approval of a loan and disbursement of it is largely controlled by the borrower, who must take the necessary steps to fulfill the terms and conditions of the loan authorization. Delays sometimes occur because the borrower has difficulty establishing acceptable title to real estate, or obtaining delivery of machinery or construction materials, or other reasons. Borrowers are customarily allowed 4 months in which

to meet the loan requirements, and extensions of this period may be granted if justified by the circumstances. 

As of June 30, 1958, disbursements made by banks or by the Small Business Administration on Agency-approved business loans amounted to $364, 981, 000 out of a total net commitment of $450, 527,000. The Agency's share of this net commitment amount was $377, 809, 000.

Cancellations 

Of the 11, 110 business loans approved since start of the

Agency's lending program, 1,500 have been canceled in full prior to disbursement. Cancellations occur primarily for these reasons: 1. 

The borrower made other arrangements for funds; 2. 

The borrower found that he could get along without the loan; 3. 

The borrower was unable or unwilling to comply with the loan conditions; 4. 

The borrower's financial condition deteriorated to the extent that the loan could not be disbursed. 

Repayments 

As against the total of $365.0 million cited previously as having been disbursed, $104.5 million had been credited on the 

Agency's records in the form of repayments and other credits (including loans sold and deferred participation agreements terminated) through June 30, 1958. The outstanding balance on that date therefore was $260. 5 million. 

Delinquencies, Charge-offs, and Liquidation Activity 

As previously stated, disbursements had been made on 

8,135 business loans in an amount of $364, 981, 000, as of June 30, 1958. Of these, 236 loans were in a liquidation status on June 30, 1958, and 225 had payments due which were delinquent in excess of 

60 days. At December 31, 1957, 114 loans were in liquidation and

157 loans had payments due which were delinquent more than 60 days. In most instances, adequate collateral has protected the 

Government's interest. Liquidation actions have thus far resulted in the charge-off by the Small Business Administration of $219,989 of principal on 22 loans. The Agency analyzes each delinquent loan in a sympathetic manner, with a view toward assisting the borrower in overcoming his difficulties and at the same time protecting the 

Government's investment.

Hospital, Convalescent and Nursing Home Loans The Small Business Administration will make loans to hospitals and to convalescent and nursing homes which are privately owned and operated for profit, for the construction of new facilities, expansion or improvement of existing facilities or for working capital. As a means of furthering the Agency's program of assistance to hospitals and convalescent and nursing homes in obtaining necessary financing, Agency representatives had several recent meetings with officials of the American Nursing Home Association, the Department of Health, Education and Welfare, and other groups.

Fisheries Loans The Small Business Administration processes, disburses, and services loans approved by the Secretary of the Interior from the Fisheries Loan Fund established under Section 4 of the Fish and Wildlife Act of 1956. Loans may be approved by the Secretary for financing and refinancing of fishing operations; for maintenance, repair and replacement of fishing gear and vessels, and for research into the basic problems of fisheries. 

Several meetings have been held recently by Agency and Department of the Interior officials to develop improved procedures for making such loans.

Loans under the program have been of substantial assistance to fishermen, by enabling them to repair and improve their equipment and to liquidate burdensome short-term indebtedness. 

At the same time, the loans have benefited small business concerns to which the fishermen were indebted for supplies or services. In many instances, loans were used, in part, to pay off long-standing obligations of the fishermen to these small firms.

Through June 30, 1958, the Small Business Administra tion had processed 454 applications under this program. In the last 6 months 80 applications were received, of which 26 were approved by the Secretary of the Interior, 24 were declined and the remainder had been withdrawn or were pending at the end of the fiscal year.

Pool Loans 

The Small Business Administration was authorized in 1955 to make loans to corporations formed and capitalized by groups of small business concerns for the purpose of producing or securing raw materials or supplies. The loans are limited to $250,000 multiplied by the number of small businesses which have established the corporation. To date, two such pool loan applications have been received and both have been approved. 

It is believed that there are many industries which could use this type of loan with benefit but which have not yet taken advantage of the program. With this in mind, each regional director of the Agency has been asked to determine what industries, if any, in his region, might be assisted through pool loans and to recommend the best ways of keeping them informed of the program. The usefulness of the program will be expanded by new legislation which will make it clear that a business loan may be made to a group formed to obtain raw materials, equipment, inventory or supplies for the use of the members of the group. 

Development Credit Corporations 

Development corporations, aside from those organized privately, are of two types: Statewide development credit corporations, and local industrial foundations or local development corporations. Both types are growing in importance as sources of financing for small business, and both are of particular interest to the Small Business Administration. The Agency has made loans to development corporations when the proceeds of the loans were to be used to assist small business firms which would themselves be eligible for Agency loans under the Small Business Act and the Loan 

Policy Statement. 

Congress had under consideration in this period legislation which would increase Small Business Administration financial

assistance to development corporations and would thus stimulate the flow of long-term loans and equity capital to small business. 

The Small Business Administration has approved 17 

loans to local development corporations for a total of $1, 379, 000.

Transfer of Reconstruction Finance Corporation Loans Under the President's Reorganization Plan No. 1 of 

1957, the Administrator of the Small Business Administration assumed responsibility on July 1, 1957, for the administration and liquidation of the remaining small business and disaster loans of the Reconstruction Finance Corporation. This transfer included 

Reconstruction Finance Corporation "pool" loans serviced by banks; other loans serviced by banks under Loan Servicing Agreements; loans and receivables formerly serviced by the Reconstruction Finance Corporation in its Washington office, and collateralpurchased items arising from liquidation of loans. 

Urban Development Program 

In accordance with a recommendation of the Subcommittee on Urban Renewal and Small Business of the Cabinet Committee on Small Business, the Small Business Administration is working closely with other Federal agencies to assist small business concerns affected by urban renewal projects. The Small Business Administration and these other agencies seek to alleviate some of the relocation problems which confront small business concerns that are being displaced by urban renewal projects. 

The Agency's regional offices have provided information on the Agency's financial programs to offices of the Urban Renewal Administration, Housing and Home Finance Agency, and are determining with these offices the ways in which the Small Business Administration can best serve small firms adversely affected by urban renewal projects. The Agency's field offices also have been authorized to have representatives meet with local public agency officials and businessmen concerned by such projects. 

Rural Development Program 

The Small Business Administration is playing a major role in the Rural Development Program, which is intended to open wider the doors of opportunity for people in the Nation's underdeveloped

Many Small Business Administration business loans are to small firms located in rural areas. These small businesses process and distribute farm commodities and livestock, and furnish needed equipment and supplies to the farmers and stockmen. Consequently, the loans help the small firms and their communities directly, and nieghboring farmers and stockmen indirectly. Other loans help provide off-the-farm employment for members of the agricultural community.

Disaster Loans 

The Small Business Administration is authorized to make low-interest-rate loans to assist in repairing or replacing homes and businesses which have been damaged or destroyed by floods, storms or other natural disasters. The loans are made in areas declared disaster areas by the Administrator of the Small Business Administration.

The Agency is also authorized to make loans to small business concerns which have suffered substantial economic injury because of drought or excessive rainfall in their areas. These loans are made in areas designated by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture. 

Comparisons of this activity for the last 3 fiscal years are shown in the chart and table on page 51. 

Agency Declarations 

When a disaster such as a storm or a flood strikes, representatives of the Agency's field offices are on the site within a few hours in order that a full report and recommendation may be made quickly to the Administrator of the Agency. In some instances, temporary field offices are established so that disaster victims can readily talk over their losses with financial specialists and file loan applications.

In its disaster loan activities, the Small Business Administration co-operates closely with local public officials, The American Red Cross, civil defense authorities, banks, chambers of commerce and business groups. 

Between January 1 and June 30, 1958, the Agency de clared disaster areas for loan eligibility purposes as shown on page 53. 

Through December 31, 1957, the Agency had issued disaster declarations involving approximately 135 areas. Including the disasters declared in this 6-months period, disaster declarations have been issued covering areas in 47 of the 48 States, as well as in Guam, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico.

Drought Disaster Loans 

The Small Business Administration provides financial assistance to small business concerns located in drought areas if the concerns have suffered substantial economic injury as a result of the drought condition. Banks in these areas are co-operating with the Agency by accepting drought loan applications and by obtaining credit information needed in evaluating loan requests. In addition, the Agency has arranged for local advisory committees, composed of bankers and businessmen with intimate knowledge of local conditions, to assist in the consideration of applications. 

For the most part, loans granted are confined to working capital, although refinancing of indebtedness (except bank loans) on which delinquencies are directly attributable to the drought is permissible. Loans may also be used to replenish normal inventories but cannot be made for expansion of facilities. 

Through June 30, 1958, the Agency had approved 185 drought disaster loans for $3, 953, 000.

Excessive Rainfall Disaster Loans 

On February 22, 1958, the President signed into law legislation authorizing the Small Business Administration to make disaster loans to small business concerns which have suffered substantial economic injury because of excessive rainfall in their areas. 

The areas must be declared major disaster areas by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture.

The criteria for approval of these loans are similar to requirements in the drought disaster loan program, and the loans may be used for the same purposes as the drought loans. 

At the time the new excessive rainfall loan authority was given the Agency, exceptionally heavy rainfall had been occurring for several months in certain areas of the country particularly the Southern and Southwestern sections. As soon as the Agency was authorized to accept applications under this program, it was notified by the Department of Agriculture that 608 counties in 17 States had been declared major disaster areas because of excessive rainfall. 

These totals had been increased by June 30, 1958, to 738 counties in 21 States.

A total of 826 applications for excessive rainfall loans, requesting $18,845, 000, were received from February 22 through June 30, 1958. This resulted in 489 approved loans for $7, 474, 000 during this period.

Bank Co-Operation in Disaster Loan Program 

Co-operation by private financial institutions in the 

Agency's Disaster Loan Program has been very gratifying. Banks in the disaster areas, or in neighboring towns, have customarily co-operated fully with the Agency's financial specialists, and frequently have furnished personnel to assist applicants in the preparation of credit data and the necessary forms. 

Disaster Loan Disbursements and Delinquencies 

As of June 30, 1958, net commitments on disaster loans, after cancellations of loans, totaled $73, 121, 000. The Small Business Administration's share was $70, 118, 000. Disbursements totaled $65, 915, 000. 

At June 30, 1958, borrowers were delinquent in excess of 60 days on 394 loans, with delinquent installments totaling $1, 143, 000. Only 34 disaster loans, involving a principal amount of $149,589, had been charged off.

Status of Small Business Revolving Fund 

Appendix C of this report presents financial statements pertaining to the operations of the business and disaster financial assistance programs.

Pursuant to legislative authorizations, appropriations totaling $340 million have been made to the revolving fund which finances these programs. These appropriations, supplemented by interest, fees, and miscellaneous income, represent the total available to finance the Small Business Administration's share of loans, administrative expenses of the programs, and interest payable to the Treasury on the net amount expended from the revolving fund.

1/ With respect to deferred participation loans, includes an amount equivalent to 20 per cent of the net amount of such loans approved after deducting actual purchases by Small Business Administration. 

Loans may be approved and disbursed on a cumulative basis in an amount greater than the total funds made available by appropriation and by income. This is because repayments on loans previously made are available for relending, through the revolving

fund operation. Moreover, in the case of deferred participation loans, disbursements are made by the participating banks, under a Small Business Administration guarantee to purchase an agreed share of the outstanding loan balance any time the bank may request. 

Experience indicates the Agency will be called upon to purchase a relatively small proportion of its share of these loans. Accordingly, in the case of business loans of this type, funds are presently committed to the extent of only 20 per cent of the Small Business Administration share. In relation to this rate, actual purchases to date have amounted to slightly more than 10 per cent. 

The table on page 56 reflects the extent of Government funds committed or disbursed in carrying out the Financial Assistance Program. Since the figures pertain only to the Government's share of loans and commitments outstanding, the table does not reflect the total financial assistance made available by the Agency and by participating banks. For the business loan program, the tabulation on page 58 is designed to show from the standpoint of applicant-borrowers not only the bank share of the total but also the extent of financial assistance which has been made possible through the revolving fund type of operation. 

Statutory Limitation on Outstanding Loans and Commitments 

Although appropriations to the revolving fund total only $340 million based on actual needs through June 30, 1958, as of that date the Congress had authorized appropriations for the loan programs in an amount not to exceed $430 million. Thus, not to exceed $305 million is authorized for business loans and commitments, and not to exceed $125 million is authorized for disaster loans and commitments. Since these limitations relate only to the Government's share of outstanding loans and commitments and do not pertain to the share of participating banks, the total amount of outstanding loans and commitments could exceed the stipulated amounts. 

As of June 30, 1958, loans and commitments chargeable against the $305 million authorized for business loans aggregated $290.9 million. As of the same date, similar charges against the $125 million statutory limitation on disaster loans totaled $35.1 million.

resulted in a cumulative deficit of $21, 289, 458, including a loss of $7,084, 508 for the fiscal year ended June 30, 1958. Part of this deficit is attributable to disaster lending operations, and part to business lending. Since by statute the maximum interest rate on disaster loans is only 3 per cent, and since interest expense to Treasury on the net amount expended from the revolving fund was payable during the fiscal year at a rate of 3-3/4 per cent, it is obvious that the disaster program will show a deficit, even without taking into account administrative expenses and provision for estimated losses.

For some items, such as interest and fee income, separate accounts are maintained by the Agency for the business and disaster lending operation. On the other hand, it would be extremely difficult and expensive to maintain an accounting system which would permit an exact separation of all administrative expense costs between the two programs. However, through a combination of accounts and statistics, it is possible to develop a distribution of each income and expense item between the two programs. This distribution, while it must be qualified as partially estimated, is deemed to be reasonably accurate and sufficiently valid for present purposes. On this basis, the tabulation on page 60 reflects a distribution of the net loss incurred during the period July 1, 1957, through June 30, 1958.

With respect to the table, income was derived from (1) interest on business loans receivable at an average rate slightly below 6 per cent, (2) interest on disaster loans receivable at 3 per cent, and (3) fees on deferred participation loans disbursed by banks at an average rate slightly in excess of 1 per cent (considering the reduction in fees as discussed earlier in this report). On funds disbursed from the revolving fund, the interest payment to Treasury for the fiscal year was 3-3/4 per cent. For business loans this left a margin of approximately 2-1/4 per cent to partially offset the expense of processing applications and closing and servicing loans approved, and the provision for estimated future losses. For disaster loans, there was an immediate deficit of 3/4 per cent, without considering expenses and provision for future losses. Since no Government funds have been disbursed on deferred participation loans outstanding, the approximate 1 per cent revenue was available to offset in part the expenses involved in processing applications and administering the loans approved.

The provision for estimated losses represents a charge of 1.8 per cent on all new business loans and 5.4 per cent on all new disaster loans disbursed during the period. This provision, which is based on Government lending agency experience factors developed over a period of years, is made in order to accumulate reserves for possible future losses. Sufficient experience has not yet been gained to test the validity of these rates with respect to the types of loans made by Small Business Administration. Business

CHAPTER IV 

GOVERNMENT PROCUREMENT ASSISTANCE Of 

In the fiscal year which ended June 30, 1958, the Small Business Administration and co-operating Government purchasing agencies set aside 17, 819 proposed Government purchases totaling more than $1 billion for award to small business concerns. this number, 12, 015 procurements, totaling $683, 883, 000, were set aside for small business in this reporting period January through June 1958. Both in number and in dollar value, the setasides were the greatest for any 6-months period and fiscal year since start of the Agency. 

By comparison, in fiscal year 1957, the Small Business Administration and co-operating Government purchasing agencies set aside 11, 851 proposed purchases, totaling $744, 335, 000, for award to small firms. Of these 7,062 purchases amounting to $399,061,000, were set aside in the period January through June 1957. The small business set-aside program is conducted by the Small Business Administration in co-operation with the Department of Defense and civilian purchasing agencies. Accomplishments in the program therefore represent the combined efforts of a number of Government agencies working together in behalf of small business.

The table and charts on pages 62 and 63 show small business set-asides in the January-June period of fiscal years 1954 through 1958. It is noteworthy that in each year increases were shown in both the number and value of purchases which the Small Business Administration proposed be reserved for award to small business concerns, purchases which the procurement agencies agreed to earmark for small business, and resulting contract awards to small firms. 483267 O-58-5 61

Highlights of Other Accomplishments 

In addition to carrying on the set-aside program, the Small Business Administration in the past 6 months performed many other services to help small business firms sell to the 

Government. 

1. 

The Agency's field offices reported 16, 268 cases of aid to small firms on general procurement matters, counseling of small business owners and managers on selling to the Government, and assistance to individual small business concerns in connection with specific procurement matters. 

2. There were 4, 401 cases in which the Agency helped small concerns obtain the opportunity to bid on specific 

Government purchases. 

3. The Agency furnished Government contracting offices the names of 5, 695 potential small business suppliers of items and services for which additional competition was desired. 

4. Small Business Administration specialists made 

1,479 calls on prime contractors to develop subcontracting work for small business concerns. 

5. As a result of these calls on prime contractors, the Agency was able to refer 4, 493 subcontracting opportunities to small firms. 

6. The Agency referred to small business concerns which had requested its assistance 17, 480 opportunities to bid. on Government purchases suitable to their production facilities. 

7. The Agency issued the "U. S. Government Pur chasing and Specifications Directory," through which small firms can learn where and how to get on bidders lists for items or services they can furnish to the Government and where to obtain or review specifications needed in preparing their bids or in deciding whether to bid on particular purchases.

The Small Business Set-Aside Program 

When an

To carry out this program the Small Business Administration has assigned representatives to the major Government purchasing offices. There, in co-operation with procurement officials, they screen proposed purchases to determine whether small firms can supply the needed items or services. 

Agency representative finds that a purchase could be supplied by small firms and that there would be a sufficient number of small business bidders to insure adequate competition, he recommends that all or part of the purchase be made exclusively from small firms on the basis of competitive bids. 

as follows: 

The rules governing set-asides for small business are When an entire purchase is set aside for exclusive award to small business, bidding for the contract is limited to firms which qualify as "small. " If a large concern submits a bid, the bid is considered "nonresponsive, " as not meeting the stipulated requirements. Where part of a proposed purchase is set aside for award to small business, in order to be considered in negotiation of a contract for that part of the purchase, a small firm must (1) bid on the unreserved portion of the purchase, that is, the part that is open to all bidders, large and small and (2) its bid on the unreserved portion must be within 120 per cent of the highest unit price at which the award is made for that portion. 

In negotiating with small firms that have met these requirements, the purchasing office starts with the one which submitted the lowest bid on the unreserved portion. 

When only one firm receives an award on the unreserved portion, the portion set aside for small business must be obtained at the same unit price.

When the unreserved portion is obtained through multiple awards, the set-aside portion is obtained at the highest unit price paid for the unreserved portion.

The set-aside program assures that suitable purchases will be earmarked for exclusive award to small business. Furthermore, when part of a proposed purchase is set aside for small business, the opportunity for small firms to receive awards is doubled; if small firms fail to obtain contracts for the unreserved portion, they still may obtain them for the set-aside portion. Moreover, small firms are given the opportunity to bid or quote on smaller quantities, since a partial set-aside divides the purchase into at least two parts.

As time would permit, Agency representatives also have maintained liaison with 140 other purchasing centers which do a lesser volume of purchasing. In order to provide the greatest assistance to small firms with the personnel available, the Agency's main effort has been concentrated in the larger purchasing installations.

The table on page 67 shows the results of the set-aside program in the past 6 months by Department or Agency with which the Small Business Administration has an active program. 

Here are some of the items called for in purchases which the Small Business Administration was able to have set aside for small business in the past 6 months by locating competent small business firms which wished to bid on the purchases:

Procurement Assistance and Counseling 

11 The Small Business Administration helps small firms determine which items or services they can supply to the Government and which agencies buy them, assists the firms in obtaining

listing on the appropriate bidders lists, and helps them obtain bid sets for purchases they are able to supply. The Agency is giving particular emphasis to informing small firms interested in doing research and development work how and where to present detailed information about their fields of interest, their research scientists, development engineers and other technical personnel, and their laboratory equipment and research facilities. 

The Small Business Administration also assists small firms in solving problems regarding bids and specifications and helps resolve difficulties between small firms and Government procurement officials.

In order to provide small firms as full and current information about Government purchasing as possible, the Small 

Business Administration has revised its "U. S. Government Pur chasing Directory" and "U. S. Government Specifications Directory" and published the two in combined form under the title "U. S. Government Purchasing and Specifications Directory. " The new and expanded directory provides detailed information on the items and services bought by the Federal Government and the Agencies which buy them; sources of specifications used by the Agencies in their purchasing; steps to take in locating prime contract and subcontract opportunities; and other helpful data. The directory, which is widely used both in industry and Government, is sold by the Superintendent of Documents at 55 cents a copy.

Many Small Business Administration field offices display a limited number of bid sets for current Government purchases which can be supplied by small business, and inform interested small firms how copies of the sets and specifications and drawings relating to them can be obtained.

In the 6 months which ended June 30, 1958, Small Business Administration field offices reported 16, 268 cases of counseling of small firms on selling to the Government or representation of small firms at purchasing offices in connection with procurement matters. 

In addition, 4, 401 firms were helped to bid on specific procurements.
