Increasing Small Business Participation 

As a supplement to the set-aside program, Small Business Administration representatives at Government procurement centers and the Agency's field offices co-operate in locating competent small business suppliers of items or services for which small business competition is inadequate or lacking.

The primary aim of this program is to increase the number and variety of items and services on which joint setasides for small business may be made. The program also serves to broaden the Government's procurement base and to assure a better price to the Government because of greater competition. Even when furnishing of additional small business suppliers does not result in set-asides for small business, it gets small firms on the proper bidders lists so that they may bid on future procurements. In the period January-June 1958, the 

Small Business Administration provided Government contracting officers the name of 5, 695 potential suppliers. A few of the items set aside for small business due to this action are shown on page 66.

The Agency's work in this field is illustrated by the following case:

The Small Business Administration representative at a large purchasing office and the officials of that office began a co-operative effort to reduce the number of "sole source procurements," that is, purchases which were being made from single companies. While screening such a procurement, the Agency's representative found that although there was only one known source (a large business) of the item, and no Agency specifications or drawings for it, the item could be made by small business. Further investigation disclosed that it was not a proprietary item and that the sole source firm's drawings were on file at the purchasing office. Since the Government had the rights to these drawings, the 

Small Business Administration asked that the entire purchase be set aside for small business. The purchasing office agreed to do 

To insure adequate small business participation, the Small Business Administration and the procurement officials, acting separately, then furnished drawings of the item to more than 200 So.

small business firms to learn if they were interested in bidding on it.

This resulted in bids from 23 small firms and award of the contract to a small firm at a unit price which was only about one-fourth that previously charged by the sole source producer. 

Subcontracting Program 

Because of a shift in the nature of defense buying, with greater emphasis on missiles, aircraft and other highly complex weapons purchased under the "weapons system concept," the importance of subcontracting to small business concerns has increased, and apparently will continue to increase. 

The Small Business Administration therefore is broadening and itensifying its Subcontracting Program, tailoring it to the needs of small firms under changed procurement conditions. 

For example, the Agency recently made available to its field offices and its procurement center representatives, as well as to the military services and Congressional committees, a "Missile

Subcontracting" directory, which contains (a) the names of items making up ballistic missiles, guided missiles, rockets, drones and targets and (b) a list of companies holding contracts for the production of sub-systems, components, assemblies, sub-assemblies and parts of the missiles, rockets, and so forth. This directory can be reviewed by interested persons at any field office of the Agency.

To carry out its Subcontracting Program, the Agency compiles data on the facilities of small firms which have asked assistance in obtaining subcontracts. Through use of this data, the Agency is able to recommend interested subcontractors to firms requiring additional production facilities. 

Major prime contractors that have agreed to establish small business subcontracting programs in co-operation with the 

Department of Defense are consulted by Small Business Admin istration representatives to determine which component parts will be subcontracted. Through its facilities records, the Agency is then able to recommend small firms interested in bidding on these subcontracting opportunities. 

The Small Business Administration and the Atomic 

Energy Commission also have established a co-operative

subcontract program to help small firms obtain subcontracts from the Atomic Energy Commission's cost-type contractors. 

In developing subcontracting opportunities for small business, Small Business Administration representatives made 

1,479 calls on major prime contractors in the past 6 months.

As a result, the Agency was able to refer 4, 493 subcontracting opportunities to small firms. Statistics on contract awards resulting from this program are not available, since the referrals made by the Small Business Administration often result, over a period of years, in multiple contract awards which are not reported.

Research and Development Contracts 

Statistics indicate that in fiscal year 1957, military research and development contracts awarded to small business amounted to only 4. 3 per cent of the value of such contracts. 

This small percentage of awards to small firms indicates that the scientific knowledge and engineering capacity of small firms are not being utilized by the Government to the fullest desirable extent.

In contracting for research and development work, Federal agencies seek participation by the best qualified firms, educational institutions, nonprofit organizations, or individuals in order to receive the best products or studies. This policy places a premium on experience and on superiority of facilities. 

The Agency asks that purchases of applied research and development projects be set aside for small business whenever two or more small business firms are considered qualified to carry out the project.

Small Business Administration field offices counsel and guide small firms and individuals interested in research and development work and direct them to the appropriate procurement officials. The Agency's field offices also locate additional small concerns interested in bidding on research and development projects when notified by Agency representatives at procurement centers that greater small business competition is needed.

The Small Business Administration recently issued a Management Aid on "Research and Development Opportunities in the Federal Government" which should prove helpful to small firms desiring such work. This publication describes the steps to be taken by those seeking research and development work with the Government, lists the types of research and development projects contracted for by civilian agencies, and tells which of their offices should be contacted by interested small firms. The Small 

Business Administration also has compiled a list of 1, 100 small firms interested in performing research and development work, including their specific fields of interest. These lists have been furnished to Government purchasing offices for use in contacting small firms when projects suitable to their facilities are being considered.

Prime Contract Referral Program 

When a small business concern asks assistance in obtaining Government orders and contracts, the Small Business Administration obtains from it data about its production facilities. 

The Agency then notifies the firm of current bidding opportunities which are suited to its particular type of equipment. This service is provided until the firm has had time to get on the bidders list and receive notice of the future purchases direct from the purchasing installations. Since the Agency's field offices obtain bid sets from the principal Government purchasing offices, they are able to notify small firms of many bidding opportunities of which the firms otherwise would be unaware. 

In the past 6 months, the Small Business Administration gave 17, 480 notifications of current bidding opportunities to small business concerns which had requested such assistance. 

Prime Contract Authority 

The Small Business Administration is empowered to enter into contracts with any Department or Agency of the United States Government to furnish supplies and services and to arrange for the performance of such contracts through subcontracting to small business concerns or others, as may be necessary. This prime contracting function is on a standby basis for use in an extraordinary emergency.

Review of Government Procurement Policies and Procedures 

As a result of a recommendation by the President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business, a task force was established in the General Services Administration to review procurement policies and procedures in order to increase small business participation in Government contracts. The Small Business Administration has maintained close liaison with the task force and has had Agency representatives serving on its staff. With recent reorganization of the task force into an interagency group, the Small Business Administration now has full-time representation on the task force and its staff. 

Among their recommendations for legislation were (1) extension of the criteria for the set-aside program to assure a fair share of Government purchases to small business, and (2) authorization for classes of Federal contracts to be set aside. for small business concerns without the necessity for considering every individual contract. These recommendations have been incorporated into section 15 of the new Small Business Act. 

The task force now has under way a study of 55 procurement problems. Study groups or special projects have been organized to develop regulations or other measures to solve these problems. At the end of June reports had been submitted on 13 of these problems with the remaining reports scheduled for completion this year.

CHAPTER V PRODUCTION AND MANAGEMENT ASSISTANCE 

During the 6-months period covered by this report, the Small Business Administration provided small business concerns a wide range of production and management assistance. 

Certificates of Competency The Small Business Administration provides an appeal procedure for small firms which face loss of Government contract awards because the contracting officer believes the firms lack the necessary financial or productive capacity. The Agency is authorized by the Small Business Act to certify that small firms or groups of small firms are competent, both financially and from a productive capacity standpoint, to perform specific Government contracts. 

Once such a certificate is issued by the Agency, the procurement officer is directed by law to accept the Agency's determination as conclusive, as far as the financial and technical capabilities of the applicant are concerned. 

Certificates of competency are issued by the Small Business Administration under the following conditions: 1. 2. 3.

The small firm or the group of small firms applying for a certificate has submitted the lowest responsive bid on an advertised purchase or has submitted a proposal on a negotiated purchase that is within price negotiation range. 

The contracting officer making the purchase has questioned the firm's financial ability or productive capacity in relation to the proposed contract.

The Small Business Administration has deter mined from a comprehensive study of the firm's facilities, manufacturing experience, technical 77 483267 O-58-6

ability, and financial capabilities that the firm has the capacity and credit to perform the contract. 

After a contract has been awarded as the result of issuance of a certificate of competency, a Small Business Administration representative follows closely the small firm's performance. If it experiences production or other difficulties, the Agency assists with the problems. 

During the last 6 months, 147 applications for certificates of competency were received by the Small Business Administration and 75 certificates were issued. The table on page 79 shows the number of applications received, the purchasing agency involved and the number of certificates issued since start of the Agency.

During fiscal year 1958, the Small Business Administration received 239 applications for certificates of competency relating to Government purchases involving $62, 562, 724. After investigation, the Agency issued 115 certificates covering procurements amounting to $26, 077, 581.

In many other cases Small Business Administration specialists assisted small companies to gain contract awards without use of the certificate of competency procedure. For example, where a contracting officer notifies the Small Business Administration that a small concern is low bidder on a procurement, but does not appear to have the financial or productive capacity to perform the contract, the Agency may develop additional facts that indicate to the contracting officer that the firm can do the job. The contracting officer may then award the contract to the small concern without formal issuance of a certificate of competency. 

Here are two recent certificate of competency cases which illustrate how the program is carried out, and its value to small firms:

A small business concern in Indiana was the low bidder in the amount of $46, 171. 25, on 7, 100 truck-end duck curtains.

The procurement agency questioned the capacity of the company

An electronic equipment parts manufacturer on Long Island was rejected as the low bidder on cable assemblies on the grounds that it lacked the necessary skill to fulfill the contract. 

A Small Business Administration certificate of competency was issued after a review of the company's capabilities. The resulting contract was completed satisfactorily, with part of the production being shipped 30 days ahead of schedule and the remainder on schedule.

Technical Production Assistance 

Many small business concerns do not have employees with the specialized technical experience needed to solve production problems. Furthermore, when small concerns undertake defense contracts or production of items that differ from their normal lines, these problems frequently increase. Small Business Administration field staff specialists are available to help such firms solve their production difficulties.

Frequently, small firms are assisted by the Agency in acquiring basic materials and production equipment, or are guided on plant layout, methods engineering, and related problems. The wide scope of the assistance given by the Agency's field offices is indicated in these examples taken from regional office reports: 

1. A small company had obtained a $100, 000 loan from the Small Business Administration to use in carrying out Government prime contracts and subcontracts. The firm met with considerable difficulty in obtaining materials and parts, and this slowed down and even at one point completely halted -- its operations. The Small Business Administration assisted the company to obtain the materials and parts so that it could continue in operation. 

2. An operator of a small drayage firm visited a Small 

Business Administration field office to discuss possible ways of improving his firm's operations. After reviewing the company's

operations, Small Business Administration specialists made suggestions which were put into effect at once. Within 3 months the businessman reported an increase in both volume of business and profits. 

3. A western producer of charcoal had been experimenting unsuccessfully with the manufacture of briquettes from powdered charcoal. When he sought the Small Business 

Administration's help the Agency suggested that he try a new special binder. The Agency has been informed that pilot runs have been made by the manufacturer with considerable success. 

4. During an inspection of a small new oak flooring manufacturer, Small Business Administration specialists noticed that wood coming off the inspection conveyor was being placed in conventional stationary grading racks. The specialists suggested that the stationary grading racks be replaced by wheeled rack sections which could easily be moved when full. The sug- gestion was adopted, with a resulting reduction in handling costs and an improvement in work flow. 

5. Production specialists of the Agency assisted a manufacturer of occasional tables. The company had a good sales record, but inventories of slower moving items piled up. 

Assistance to this company resulted in curtailing the manufacture of slow-moving items and decreasing the inventory by storage of unfinished furniture until needed to meet customer requirements. Small Business Administration specialists also analyzed production flow and discovered a method of arranging a minimum production run of 4 hours, rather than 16 hours, for any one item.

This change of production scheduling helped decrease the inventory. 

6. The Small Business Administration assisted a small defense contractor obtain extension on a lease of Government-owned machine tools. This enabled his firm to retain a number of production workers who would otherwise have been released and made possible completion of two contracts for the Air Force. 

7. A small aircraft maintenance and overhaul firm was low bidder on a maintenance and overhaul contract. 

However, the purchasing office questioned the adequacy of the firm's facilities. Small Business Administration representatives

suggested a way in which existing equipment could be rearranged so that the firm not only could handle the proposed contract but also take care of its commercial work. The procurement officials agreed that the firm could meet production requirements and the award was made. 

8. A well drilling contractor was assisted in obtaining argon gas when an acute shortage of this material threatened to shut down his work temporarily. 

9. A small ready-mix concrete plant was considering setting up a branch operation in a nearby town. The owner and his banker asked the Small Business Administration to assist in determining plant size and layout, and to consider participating with the bank in a loan for the project. A Small Business Administration representative assisted the owner in obtaining data on present and potential markets and related cost factors. Analysis of the data disclosed that the proposal was unsound, inasmuch as the pay-out period would be longer than the proposed economic life of the installation. The company discarded the expansion plan, which could have resulted in a serious financial loss. 

Inventory of Small Plant Facilities The Small Business Administration's Facilities Inventory is a nationwide program for compiling data on the production facilities of small business. Small firms are asked to register with the 

Agency's field offices, which are responsible for maintaining the inventory.

The inventory is used (1) to assist registered firms in obtaining prime contract and subcontract opportunities; (2) to furnish additional bidders to Government contracting offices; (3) to locate specialized or scarce machine tools and equipment; and (4) to provide data for defense mobilization planning. 

During the past 6 months this activity has concentrated on registering qualified small business concerns for research and development work.

This is being done so that defense and civilian agencies engaged in this work will know of these facilities for peacetime or mobilization needs, and to permit such work to be channeled to small business. 

As of June 30, 1958, 38, 373 small companies had registered their facilities with the Agency.

Materials and Equipment 

The last 6 months have been marked by a plentiful supply of basic materials for small business, as a result of the economic readjustment. Even the supply of nickel has expanded beyond immediate needs, thus eliminating the one situation of short supply which was present in the previous reporting period. 

Although the supply of basic materials and equipment remains favorable, materials and equipment costs continue to be of concern to small firms. As a result, in this reporting period the Agency received numerous requests from small firms for help in locating commercial and Government sources of used equipment and materials. Most of these requests cited high costs of new equipment and materials as the basic reason for seeking used items. Inquiries covered a wide range of items; for example, surplus Government-owned fork lift trucks, and privately owned agricultural products, machine tools and marine equipment. 

The Agency acts as advocate and spokesman for small business before other Government agencies which are considering the adoption or revision of materials and equipment policies, programs or regulations. During the last 6 months, Small Business Administration representatives have taken part in various conferences with representatives of other agencies on the proposed disposal of surplus Government-owned vessels and aircraft. 

Also, at the request of the Office of Defense and Civilian Mobilization, the Small Business Administration participated in an interagency task group to develop guidelines for selecting Governmentowned machine tools to be added to the National Industrial Equipment Reserve. 

The Office of Defense and Civilian Mobilization asked the Agency to review a number of requests from the Department of Defense for approval to lease Government-owned machine tools for nondefense purposes. The Agency looks at such proposed leases to make certain they will not adversely affect the competitive position of small concerns. When a proposed lease might have this effect, the Agency makes appropriate recommendations to the Office of Defense and Civilian Mobilization. 

Counsel and aid to small firms on the Government's materials and equipment regulations is provided through the Small Business Administration regional and branch offices.

Tax Amortization Assistance Authority of the Office of Defense and Civilian Mobiliza tion to issue certificates for rapid tax amortization of emergency facilities has been reduced under Public Law 85-165, dated 

August 26, 1957. The Office of Defense and Civilian Mobilization approved no applications for tax amortization certificates since that date. Consequently, the Small Business Administration has had little activity in tax amortization during this reporting period. 

That activity has been to obtain additional information about small firms' applications for tax amortization certificates.

Specifications Assistance 

A continuing problem of small business firms is difficulty in obtaining copies of Government specifications. Frequently, small firms receive bid sets from Government contracting offices without appropriate specifications, or with the basic specification alone and important reference specifications omitted. Often a small firm writes to the purchasing agency for specifications, but supply is exhausted.

The Small Business Administration maintains at its Washington and field offices reference sets of the packaging and packing specifications most often required by small concerns in selling their products and services to the Government. 

During this reporting period, the Agency worked closely with the Task Force for Reviewing Government Procurement Policies and Procedures. The Agency provided technical guidance to the task force study group seeking to (1) simplify Government marking, packing and packaging specifications, and (2) determine if small business suffers a disadvantage in obtaining the use of Government-owned facilities. In addition, a staff member of the 

Agency is serving on the study group dealing with the over-all area of Government specifications, and another is chairman of an interagency special staff which is studying ways to make specifications more readily available to businessmen. The Small Business 

Administration has submitted several problems to the study group for consideration and possible solution.

In the period January through June 1958, the Small 

Business Administration received from the General Services 

Administration 94 new or interim Federal specifications. These

were reviewed to make certain the provisions would not restrict bidding opportunities of small concerns. The Agency made several recommendations to the General Services Administration for deletion of possible restrictive or discriminatory provisions. 

The Small Business Administration also seeks the issuance of new specifications, or revision of existing ones, when this will increase the opportunities for small manufacturers to have their products considered in Government purchasing. Negotiations were held toward this end during the past year with the Department of Agriculture, Quartermaster Corps, Marine Corps, Coast Guard,

Signal Corps, National Bureau of Standards, and the General Serv ices Administration. 

ance: 

Here are some typical examples of specifications assist- A small manufacturer reported to the Small Business Administration that he had been unable to obtain Government contracts because a major purchasing office was using a purchase description which described a proprietary product of a competitor. 

The Agency analyzed copies of the bid invitations, purchase descriptions, bid prices and related data, and determined that the complainant had a valid objection. The Small Business Administration then asked the contracting officers to broaden their purchase descriptions, but this was refused. The Agency next asked the Department of Defense to prepare a military specification on the product that would have to be used by all military purchasing offices. The Department of Defense accepted the Small Business Administration's recommen dation. The Agency participated in the preparation of the specification by representatives of the Office of Standardization of the Department of Defense and the principal manufacturers of the product. In participating, the Agency sought the development of a specification that would permit interested small firms to qualify for Government purchases of the product. A broadened specification, which permitted competitive bidding, was finally issued. A small firm found that it could not compete with larger corporations for the sale of plastic assault boats because the specification provided for a molded hull requiring the use of expensive dies and equipment. The Small Business Administration was successful in having the specification changed to allow the use of alternative methods of production. The result a small firm received a contract for over $1 million. 

---

Small Business Production Pools for Defense 

The Small Business Administration is authorized to advise and assist in the formation of small business production pools and to formally approve the pools' proposed operations, subject to the concurrence of the Attorney General and the Chairman of the 

Federal Trade Commission. 

Production pooling provides to small business a means for combining their efforts thus enabling small firms to obtain contracts which would be beyond individual productive capacities. Although experience indicates that pools are more likely to be successful in wartime than in periods such as the present, interest in production pools has shown a decided increase during the last 6 months. 

Firms which have suffered a decline in orders for civilian-type items appear to be considering forming pools to improve their competitive positions in bidding on purchases.

At present there are 5 approved pools, only 1 of which considers itself active and is seeking defense prime contracts and subcontracts. The 4 other pools are maintaining their organizations on a standby basis. 

Two of the 5 approved pools have reported receiving 304 prime contract awards totaling $1,277,932, and 4 subcontract awards valued at $85, 967.

Rural Development Program The Committee for Rural Development Program, an interdepartmental committee composed of representatives of the 

Department of Agriculture, the Department of Health, Education and Welfare, the Department of Commerce, the Department of Labor, the Council of Economic Advisers and the Small Business Administration has been initiated to assist farm families having a gross income of less than $1,000 per year to improve their economic status. The objective of this Rural Development Program is to help people in rural counties and areas make the best use of their existing resources.

The Small Business Administration makes its services available to small business firms located in counties which have been designated as focal points for rural development assistance.

Through its many programs the Agency seeks to improve business conditions in the areas, aiding the local industries and co-operating with local, State and other Federal agencies to provide off-the-farm employment for members of farm families. 

Surplus Labor Area Assistance 

During the past 6 months there has been an increase in the number of surplus labor areas referred to this Agency by the 

Bureau of Employment Security, Department of Labor. That Bu reau regularly designates those major areas which are surplus labor areas and, as necessary, determines smaller areas of labor surplus. These findings are referred to the Small Business Administration for assistance to small business firms in the designated areas. Small Business Administration assistance to individual small firms and local industries and groups in surplus labor areas stresses the "building from within" approach. Emphasis is placed on helping existing small firms to expand and to diversify. This approach supplements the Area Development Program of the Depart ment of Commerce, which provides information on how to bring new industries into such areas. 

Small Business Administration production and industrial specialists meet with managers of small business concerns and community leaders in the surplus labor areas to consider means of stabilizing or increasing local employment. Studies are made of the possibility of expanding the markets for the communities' existing products, introducing additional or new products, or obtaining for the areas 

Government prime contracts or subcontracts. 

Products Assistance Program The Small Business Administration's Products Assistance 

Program has two primary objectives: 1. 

To provide information and aid to small concerns in gaining access to data on product or process research, and market development needed in solving day-to-day operating problems and in planning new product, production, and marketing programs; and

2. 

To provide for small firms a clearinghouse of information on available patented products and processes which may be of interest to them in their present operations or in di versification or expansion. 

Expenditures by both Government and industry for research and development have increased steadily over the years. As a result, much data and technological information of great value to small concerns have accumulated. Much of this information, particularly that which has been developed in Government laboratories and installations, is nonconfidential in nature and is available to interested small business owners and operators. However, many small business owners either do not have the time or do not know where to go to obtain the information required to solve a specific problem. Similarly, small companies frequently need guidance in obtaining technical information, data and services from commercial or educational sources. The Products Assistance Program bridges these gaps by helping small businesses gain access to the required information and its technical applications. 

When a small business concern submits a problem to the Small Business Administration, the Agency obtains recommended solutions from the best qualified sources in industry and the Government. The solutions suggested by these sources are consolidated by the Agency into a basic report for the inquirer. 

The Small Business Administration issues each month a Products List Circular presenting a brief digest of patented machines, devices, and processes which are available for use by small firms through purchase, licensing, or other commercial arrangement. Included in the circular are both privately owned and 

Government-owned patents.

Approximately 15, 000 small concerns now have asked to receive the Products List Circular on a continuing basis. The 

Products List Circulars published in the last 6 months listed 704 products, manufacturing processes, and materials which recently have been patented and are available for use by small firms. 

In this reporting period, the products assistance branch considered and answered 1, 762 requests for information concerning items published in the Products List Circular. In addition, 2, 649

individual small business concerns sought facts or counsel from the field representatives of the Small Business Administration on various phases of product, process and market development. 

Management Development Program The Management Development Program is an educational program cosponsored by the Small Business Administration and local educational institutions to help owners and managers of small business concerns strengthen their management abilities. It is a program designed for the executives rather than employees of small firms, and is concerned with administrative management rather than day-to-day operational subjects.

Since the inauguration of two experimental courses in the spring of 1954, the program has been improved, and is being extended rapidly by schools of business, university extension divisions, evening colleges and distributive educators. 

By June 30 of this year, 419 courses had been conducted by 155 educational institutions. Approximately 13, 400 businessmen and women have successfully met requirements for certificates of completion awarded by the educational institutions. In the 6 months ending June 30, 1958, 102 of these courses were conducted by 77 institutions. These 102 courses were attended by approximately 

3, 565 businessmen and women.

The educational institution assumes educational and financial responsibility for the courses, with the Small Business Administration serving as cosponsor. 

The enthusiasm with which the courses have been received may be judged by these typical comments, taken from written evaluation of the courses by students and business owners: covered. "To me the course was very beneficial on every subject It reassured me of the correct procedures we were taking in our business and, also, pointed out some incorrect things we were overlooking, which we intend to correct. The course was well worth the time. " "The financing knowledge picked up nancing that I couldn't have gotten otherwise. to me than the cost of the entire course. 11 helped me secure fi- 

This is worth more

To improve the Management Development Program the 

Agency continues to work with the National Council for Small Business Management Development. The general purpose of this organization is to stimulate interest in management development programs for small business, and in the development and advancement of such programs. Most members of the Council are representatives of educational institutions which have conducted administrative management courses cosponsored by this Agency. In 

June 1958 the third annual conference of the group was sponsored by the Bureau of Business Research and the School of Business Administration of the University of Pittsburgh, in co-operation with the Small Business Administration.

Management Publications The Small Business Administration management publica tions are a source of practical information for owners and managers of small concerns. In addition, university teachers of business subjects use the publications as class reference material; trade associations supply them to members; private publishers review and quote them; and professional persons serving small business for ex- ample, public accountants, consultants and lawyers advising clients. 

use them in

More than five million copies of the management publications had been distributed as of June 30, 1958.

The Agency's six series of management publications are:

Management Aids for Small Manufacturers, Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers, Small Marketers Aids, Small Business Bulletins, the Small Business Management Series, and the Aids Annuals Series. 

The Aids and the Bulletins are distributed free on request, while the Small Business Management Series and Annual volumes are sold by the Superintendent of Documents at nominal prices. 

Management Aids for Small Manufacturers 

Ninety-four of these free leaflets have been produced. 

Distribution during the 6 months ending June 30, 1958 totaled more than 274,000 copies. Recent titles include: "Research and Development Opportunities With the Federal Government," "Rating 

Your Firm's Organization, " "Wishing Won't Get Profitable New

Products," and "How Business Publications Help Small Business." 

Small Marketers Aids 

Thirty-two of these Aids have been issued thus far. They are available on request without charge. Distribution during the 

January 1 June 30, 1958 period amounted to approximately 

271,000 copies. Recent titles include: "How Big Companies Help Small Marketers," and "Methods for Improving Off-Season Sales." 

Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers 

Fifty-nine Technical Aids have been published. During the past 6 months more than 77, 500 copies were distributed. The most recent publication in this series is "Fire Fighting Equipment for Small Plants. " 

Small Business Bulletins On March 4, 1958 the Business Service Bulletins Pro gram was transferred from the Department of Commerce to the Small Business Administration. It is planned to carry on this work in the Small Business Administration through a new series known as "Small Business Bulletins. " These will be 2 to 12-page free leaflets designed to provide reference sources for small business owners and managers. The first leaflet, dealing with "Handicrafts and Home Products for Profit," is now available. In the period covered by this report, 2, 935 copies were distributed on request.

Aids Annual Series 

These volumes make back numbers of the Aids available in permanent, bound form. Each winter the Aids released 2 years before are revised and reissued as a book. Copies are sold through the Superintendent of Documents. 

To date, five volumes have been published, at prices ranging from 45 cents to $1. The most recent volume is Management Aids for Small Manufacturers: 

Annual No. 4, which contains Aids 64 through 74. Sales of the 

5 Annuals during the first half of 1958 amounted to 7,764 copies. 

Small Business Management Series 

When a needed management subject cannot be covered adequately in an Aid leaflet, a leading authority on the subject is employed on a contract basis to prepare a longer manuscript. The completed manuscript is printed by the Government Printing Office and sold by the Superintendent of Documents at a nominal price. 

Widespread acceptance of the 21 booklets published thus far is indicated by total sales during the past 6 months of 31, 648 copies. 

The most recent volume in this series is Profitable Small Plant Layout, which is 48 pages in length and sells for 25 cents. 

Total Distribution of Management Publications 

On a cumulative basis through June 30, 1958, distribution of the Agency's management publications has been as follows: 

Management Aids, 2,576, 105; Technical Aids, 870,985; Small 

Marketers Aids, 1,040, 700; Small Business Bulletins, 2, 935;

Aids, Annual Series, 68, 955; Small Business Management Series, 

595, 766; and Administrative Management Courses Books, 3, 125. 

The grand total is 5,251, 028.

The following remarks are typical of many received by the Small Business Administration concerning the various management publications. 

A production manager in Mississippi writes: "We value the information contained in the Aids sufficiently to maintain a permanent file for reference The leaflets are well written convey a remarkable amount of information. We believe that your choice of subject matter is excellent. " and.

A company president in West Virginia writes: "I think they are excellent source material for a small business man an invaluable aid in helping me make decisions and gaining more inside information on various problems in a small business. " 

A trade association executive in Missouri writes: "Hardly a month goes by that I don't send one of your publications to my entire membership . . . . I think these Aids are very excellent and I would not want to change them in any way. "' 

An air procurement district official writes: "This office is receiving Small Business Administration Aids publica tions regularly and is posting them in full view of all those who visit the Air Force Small Business Division. 

We believe the Small Business Administration is doing a fine job and we welcome your assistance in giving us this aid for the small manufacturers

Many complimentary remarks have been volun- tarily made by manufacturers, both large and small.

"' 

Business Opportunity Meetings 

During the period of this report, the Small Business Administration, in co-operation with other Federal agencies and State and local business groups, cosponsored 9 Small Business Opportunity Meetings. More than 6, 000 small business owners and managers attended these meetings and learned firsthand of the Government's buying methods, prime contract and subcontract opportunities, and helpful services available from the Small Business Administration and other Federal agencies. Nineteen meetings, attended by more than 13, 000 businessmen, were held in the fiscal year which ended June 30.

Opportunity Meetings were held during this reporting period in Worcester, Massachusetts; Shreveport, Louisiana; East 

St. Louis, Illinois; Garden City, New York; Casper, Wyoming; 

Cleveland, Ohio; Buffalo, New York; Birmingham, Alabama; and 

Corpus Christi, Texas.

At the Opportunity Meetings, information on how to sell to the Government is presented through talks by top Government purchasing officials; visual displays; sample bid sets; and exhibits of products purchased by the military and civilian Federal agencies. Each meeting also features exhibits by large prime

contractors of items for which subcontractors are being sought.

In addition, Small Business Administration specialists and Govern ment contracting officers provide on-the-spot procurement counseling and assist individual firms in obtaining listing on appropriate bidders lists.

One of the chief benefits derived from the meetings has been to bring many small subcontractors or suppliers to the attention of interested prime contractors. Both small and large firms have expressed the view that the meetings are a very effective form of prime contract and subcontract assistance to small business. 

Other Meetings 

In addition to these Business Opportunity Meetings, the Small Business Administration participated in many meetings sponsored or conducted by the Department of Defense or by chambers of commerce and other local groups. Some of these meetings had the same objectives as the ones above; others were to assist in alleviating surplus labor conditions; while others were trade association meetings and shows at which Small Business Administration programs and services were explained and businessmen counseled on management, procurement, production and financial problems. 

Conferences on Technical and Distribution Research for the Benefit of Small Business 

The first national conference of this type took place in September of 1957 in Washington, D. C. Since then, at the direc tion of President Eisenhower, the Small Business Administration has assisted in arranging additional conferences at the regional and local levels. Conferences have been held in Cleveland, Buffalo, Corvallis (Oregon), Milwaukee, Oklahoma City, Tulsa, Ann Arbor (Michigan), and St. Louis. Other meetings are scheduled for Boston and Honolulu.

Management Counseling 

Small business owners and managers who have particular problems usually want assistance on a personal basis and need it on short notice. At the same time, many of them do not know where to turn for the help they need. These two problems are intensified

by the fact that, broadly speaking, the cost of outside counsel often is prohibitive for many of these small firms. 

The Management Counseling Program, carried on by the Small Business Administration through both its Washington and field offices, is designed to provide assistance in solving problems. The service is given free to businessmen who request it and hundreds make use of it each month. 

In order to be certain that the latest reliable information is constantly available to inquirers, the Small Business Administration has established a reference library in the Washington office and is setting up smaller libraries in each of its regional offices. These libraries contain textbooks, booklets which have been publicly and privately printed, business papers, business magazines, and other helpful publications. The libraries are open to all businessmen and students of small business. 

Management Research 

The growing need for additional knowledge concerning the administration and operation of small business concerns has prompted the inauguration of a Small Business Administration management research program. One research project to be conducted over a 3-year period is now underway. This project will be a detailed study of what happens to approximately 75 small businesses during the first 2 years of their existence. The field research is being undertaken by experienced technical personnel under contract with the Small Business Administration.
